# FILE: 10_Stakeholder_Engagement_and_Communication.md

# 10 â€” Business Analysis Perspectives

## 1. Module Objectives

By the end of this module, you should be able to:

* Explain the meaning of a business analysis perspective.
* Understand how context affects business analysis work.
* Distinguish common business analysis perspectives.
* Understand Agile, Business Intelligence, Information Technology, Business Architecture, and Business Process Management perspectives.
* Identify how techniques and deliverables vary across contexts.
* Apply perspective-based thinking to business scenarios.

## 2. Introduction

A business analysis perspective is a particular way of viewing business analysis work based on the context in which it is performed.

The business need, organizational environment, delivery approach, stakeholders, and solution type influence how an analyst works.

For example, a Business Analyst working on a data warehouse may focus on data quality, integration, reporting, and analytical needs. An analyst working on an Agile software team may focus on iterative delivery, backlog refinement, user stories, and frequent stakeholder feedback.

The underlying goal remains consistent: understand needs, facilitate change, and help deliver value.

The BABOK Guide describes several perspectives that provide specialized context for business analysis:

1. Agile.
2. Business Intelligence.
3. Information Technology.
4. Business Architecture.
5. Business Process Management.

These perspectives are not mutually exclusive. One initiative may involve several of them.

## 3. Agile Perspective

### 3.1 Definition

Agile is an approach to delivering value through iterative or incremental work, collaboration, feedback, and adaptation.

In an Agile environment, requirements may evolve as stakeholders learn more about the business problem and evaluate working solutions.

The Business Analyst helps the team understand needs, clarify requirements, manage priorities, and maintain alignment with business objectives.

### 3.2 Common Characteristics

* Frequent stakeholder collaboration.
* Incremental delivery.
* Iterative refinement of requirements.
* Continuous feedback.
* Adaptation to changing needs.
* Emphasis on delivering value.
* Close cooperation between business and delivery teams.

Agile does not mean working without planning or documentation. The appropriate level of documentation depends on the context.

### 3.3 Common Techniques

* User stories.
* Product backlog refinement.
* User story mapping.
* Workshops.
* Prototypes.
* Acceptance criteria.
* Iteration reviews.
* Retrospectives.
* Prioritization.

### 3.4 User Stories

A user story commonly describes a capability from a user's perspective.

Format:

"As a [user], I want [capability], so that [benefit]."

Example:

"As a customer, I want to track my order so that I know when it will arrive."

Acceptance criteria clarify the conditions that must be satisfied.

For example:

* The customer can access the current order status.
* The displayed status reflects the latest available information.
* The customer receives an understandable message when tracking information is unavailable.

### 3.5 Backlog Prioritization

A product backlog contains work items that may be considered for delivery.

Prioritization may consider:

* Business value.
* Customer impact.
* Risk.
* Dependencies.
* Urgency.
* Effort.
* Learning opportunities.

The person accountable for product value, often the Product Owner in Scrum, is responsible for effective product backlog management. The Business Analyst may support this work depending on the organization's roles.

### 3.6 Example

A company develops a mobile banking application.

Instead of attempting to deliver every feature at once, the team releases an initial version supporting secure login and balance viewing.

Customer feedback then informs the prioritization of transfers, notifications, and additional functionality.

The analyst helps clarify requirements and assess feedback as the product evolves.

## 4. Business Intelligence Perspective

### 4.1 Definition

Business Intelligence (BI) focuses on transforming data into information that supports reporting, analysis, monitoring, and business decision-making.

Business analysis in a BI context often involves understanding data sources, information needs, metrics, reporting requirements, and analytical capabilities.

### 4.2 Common Activities

* Identifying decision-making needs.
* Defining KPIs.
* Analyzing existing reports.
* Documenting data requirements.
* Assessing data quality.
* Understanding data sources.
* Defining reporting requirements.
* Supporting dashboard design.
* Validating analytical outputs.

### 4.3 Data Quality Dimensions

Useful data quality dimensions include:

**Accuracy:** Does the data correctly represent what it is intended to represent?

**Completeness:** Are required values present?

**Consistency:** Is information represented consistently across systems?

**Timeliness:** Is the data available when needed?

**Validity:** Does the data conform to defined rules and formats?

**Uniqueness:** Are duplicate records appropriately controlled?

### 4.4 Example

A retail manager wants to understand declining sales.

The analyst identifies relevant measures, including revenue, units sold, returns, discounts, customer segments, and product categories.

The analyst then investigates data sources, defines metric calculations, and documents reporting requirements.

A dashboard may reveal that the decline is concentrated in specific product categories or customer segments.

### 4.5 Reporting Requirements

A useful reporting requirement specifies:

* Intended audience.
* Business question.
* Required measures.
* Data sources.
* Filters and dimensions.
* Reporting frequency.
* Time period.
* Access restrictions.
* Accuracy and timeliness expectations.

Weak requirement: "Create a sales dashboard."

Improved requirement: "Provide regional sales managers with weekly revenue, order volume, and return-rate measures, filterable by region and product category."

## 5. Information Technology Perspective

### 5.1 Definition

The Information Technology (IT) perspective addresses business analysis in technology-related initiatives.

These initiatives may involve application development, infrastructure, system integration, cloud migration, cybersecurity controls, or technology replacement.

The analyst connects business needs with technical possibilities and constraints.

### 5.2 Common Activities

* Identifying business and technical requirements.
* Understanding system interfaces.
* Documenting functional and non-functional requirements.
* Analyzing data flows.
* Identifying integration dependencies.
* Supporting solution evaluation.
* Clarifying operational requirements.
* Coordinating business and technical stakeholders.

### 5.3 Functional Requirements

Functional requirements describe what a solution must do.

Examples:

* Users can reset their passwords.
* The system generates monthly invoices.
* Managers can approve purchase requests.
* The application records completed transactions.

### 5.4 Non-Functional Requirements

Non-functional requirements describe qualities, constraints, or performance expectations.

Examples include:

* Performance.
* Availability.
* Security.
* Accessibility.
* Reliability.
* Maintainability.
* Scalability.
* Usability.

Example:

"The service shall maintain 99.9% monthly availability, measured according to the agreed monitoring and exclusion rules."

Non-functional requirements should be defined in measurable terms whenever appropriate.

### 5.5 System Integration

System integration connects applications, services, or data sources.

An analyst may need to identify:

* Source and destination systems.
* Data exchanged.
* Transfer frequency.
* Error handling.
* Authentication and authorization.
* Data transformation rules.
* Ownership of information.
* Monitoring requirements.

### 5.6 Example

A company moves an application to a cloud platform.

The analyst documents business continuity requirements, user access needs, data migration requirements, availability expectations, and integration dependencies.

Technical specialists determine implementation details while the analyst helps maintain alignment with business needs.

## 6. Business Architecture Perspective

### 6.1 Definition

Business Architecture provides a structured view of an organization and how its parts work together to deliver value.

It helps connect strategy with business capabilities, value streams, information, organizational structure, and initiatives.

### 6.2 Common Elements

**Business capabilities:** What the organization must be able to do.

**Value streams:** How value is created and delivered through a sequence of activities.

**Organizational structure:** How responsibilities and authority are arranged.

**Information concepts:** Important business information and relationships.

**Business strategy:** The organization's objectives and direction.

### 6.3 Capability Analysis

Capability analysis identifies what an organization can do today and what it must be able to do in the future.

Example: A logistics company may have capabilities in:

* Order management.
* Transportation planning.
* Warehouse operations.
* Shipment tracking.
* Customer service.
* Billing.

A strategic objective to provide real-time shipment visibility may require improvements in tracking, data integration, and customer communication capabilities.

### 6.4 Capability Maps

A capability map organizes an organization's capabilities into a structured view.

It can help stakeholders identify:

* Weak capabilities.
* Duplicate capabilities.
* Investment priorities.
* Strategic dependencies.
* Areas for improvement.

A capability map describes what the organization needs to do rather than necessarily specifying the detailed sequence of activities.

### 6.5 Example

A bank wants to expand its digital services.

Its strategy requires stronger digital identity verification, payment processing, fraud detection, and customer support capabilities.

Business architecture helps connect the strategic objective with these capabilities and identify which initiatives are needed.

## 7. Business Process Management Perspective

### 7.1 Definition

Business Process Management (BPM) focuses on understanding, analyzing, improving, and managing business processes.

A business process is a set of related activities that produces an outcome for a customer or another stakeholder.

BPM considers process performance, consistency, efficiency, quality, and alignment with organizational goals.

### 7.2 Process Lifecycle

A typical process improvement cycle may include:

1. Identify the process and its objectives.
2. Document the current process.
3. Measure performance.
4. Analyze bottlenecks and causes.
5. Design the improved process.
6. Implement the changes.
7. Monitor results.
8. Refine the process when necessary.

This is a practical lifecycle model; organizations may use different frameworks.

### 7.3 Common Techniques

* Process mapping.
* BPMN.
* Value stream mapping.
* Root-cause analysis.
* Process simulation.
* Performance measurement.
* Gap analysis.
* Workflow analysis.

### 7.4 Process Metrics

Common measures include:

* Cycle time.
* Throughput.
* Error rate.
* Cost per transaction.
* First-pass yield.
* Rework rate.
* Waiting time.
* Customer satisfaction.

### 7.5 Example

A company processes supplier invoices manually.

The analyst maps the process and discovers duplicate data entry, unclear approval responsibilities, and delays caused by missing information.

Potential improvements include standardized invoice submission, automated validation, clearer approval rules, and exception handling.

The analyst compares performance before and after implementation.

## 8. Comparing the Five Perspectives

| Perspective                 | Primary Focus                                       | Example Deliverable                           |
| --------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- |
| Agile                       | Iterative delivery and evolving requirements        | User stories and acceptance criteria          |
| Business Intelligence       | Data, reporting, and decision support               | KPI definitions and dashboard requirements    |
| Information Technology      | Technology-enabled change                           | Functional and non-functional requirements    |
| Business Architecture       | Strategic capabilities and organizational structure | Capability map                                |
| Business Process Management | Process performance and improvement                 | Current-state and future-state process models |

The deliverables shown are examples, not exclusive outputs. A single perspective may use many techniques and deliverables.

## 9. Combining Perspectives

Real-world initiatives often involve multiple perspectives.

Consider a manufacturer introducing predictive maintenance.

**Business Architecture:** Identifies the capability needed to improve equipment reliability.

**Business Process Management:** Examines the maintenance workflow and current sources of delay.

**Business Intelligence:** Defines equipment-performance measures and reporting requirements.

**Information Technology:** Defines sensor integration, data storage, security, and application requirements.

**Agile:** Supports iterative delivery and feedback as the solution is developed.

The analyst selects techniques according to the needs of the initiative rather than treating perspectives as isolated disciplines.

## 10. Practical Case Study

### Scenario: Digital Transformation in a Logistics Company

The company wants to reduce shipment delays and improve customer visibility.

### Business Need

Customers lack reliable information about shipment status, while operations teams rely on manual updates.

### Applying the Perspectives

**Business Architecture**

Identify shipment tracking and customer communication as capabilities requiring improvement.

**Business Process Management**

Map shipment creation, dispatch, tracking updates, exception handling, and delivery confirmation.

**Business Intelligence**

Define on-time delivery rate, average delay, tracking-data freshness, and customer inquiry volume.

**Information Technology**

Define system integrations, access controls, data requirements, availability, and notification functionality.

**Agile**

Deliver an initial tracking capability, gather user feedback, and refine later releases based on value and priorities.

### Expected Outcome

The company can measure whether the initiative improves visibility, reduces customer inquiries, and supports more reliable deliveries.

## 11. Practice Questions

### Question 1

Which perspective most directly focuses on iterative delivery and frequent feedback?

A. Business Architecture.

B. Agile.

C. Business Intelligence.

D. Business Process Management.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Agile emphasizes iterative delivery, collaboration, and adaptation.

### Question 2

A company needs dashboards showing revenue by region and product category. Which perspective is especially relevant?

A. Business Intelligence.

B. Business Architecture only.

C. Organizational recruitment only.

D. Business Process Management only.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: BI focuses on data, reporting, metrics, and decision support.

### Question 3

Which perspective helps connect strategic objectives with the capabilities an organization must possess?

A. Business Architecture.

B. Agile testing only.

C. Document archiving.

D. Interview scheduling.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Business Architecture links strategy with capabilities and organizational structure.

### Question 4

A company wants to reduce invoice processing time by identifying unnecessary steps. Which perspective is most directly relevant?

A. Business Process Management.

B. Market branding.

C. Business Architecture only.

D. Product advertising.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: BPM focuses on process analysis, performance, and improvement.

### Question 5

Which is an example of a non-functional requirement?

A. Users can submit an order.

B. Managers can approve requests.

C. The service shall meet an agreed availability target.

D. The system generates an invoice.

**Correct answer: C**

Explanation: Availability describes a quality attribute rather than a specific business function.

### Question 6

A predictive maintenance initiative needs sensor integration, equipment KPIs, and a redesigned maintenance workflow. What should the analyst conclude?

A. Only one business analysis perspective can be used.

B. Multiple perspectives can contribute to the same initiative.

C. Business analysis ends when a technology is selected.

D. Metrics are unnecessary when sensors are installed.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Complex initiatives frequently combine several perspectives.

## 12. Revision Checklist

* [ ] Explain what a business analysis perspective is.
* [ ] Describe the Agile perspective.
* [ ] Understand the Business Intelligence perspective.
* [ ] Understand the Information Technology perspective.
* [ ] Explain Business Architecture.
* [ ] Explain Business Process Management.
* [ ] Distinguish capabilities from processes.
* [ ] Distinguish functional from non-functional requirements.
* [ ] Identify useful deliverables for each perspective.
* [ ] Apply multiple perspectives to a single business initiative.

## 13. Further Study Resources

* IIBA: https://www.iiba.org/
* BABOK Guide: https://www.iiba.org/standards-and-resources/babok/
* ECBA certification: https://www.iiba.org/business-analysis-certifications/ecba/

## 14. Final Exam Reminder

Read the scenario carefully and identify its primary focus:

* Iterative delivery and adaptation: Agile.
* Data, reporting, and decision support: Business Intelligence.
* Technology-enabled change: Information Technology.
* Strategy and organizational capabilities: Business Architecture.
* Process performance and improvement: Business Process Management.

Several perspectives may apply simultaneously. Select the answer that best matches the main problem described.

# FILE: 11_Requirements_Documentation_and_Modeling.md

# 11 â€” ECBA Exam Review and Practice

## 1. Purpose

This module consolidates the foundational business analysis concepts covered in the preceding study notes and provides original practice questions.

It is intended to help candidates:

* Review essential terminology.
* Distinguish closely related concepts.
* Apply business analysis principles to scenarios.
* Identify common reasoning mistakes.
* Assess their readiness for further exam preparation.

**Important:** The questions in this module are original practice questions, not official IIBA exam questions. Verify the current ECBA exam blueprint, eligibility requirements, and exam policies using IIBA's official website.

## 2. Business Analysis Fundamentals

### 2.1 What Is Business Analysis?

Business analysis enables change in an enterprise by defining needs and recommending solutions that deliver value to stakeholders.

A Business Analyst investigates problems, identifies opportunities, facilitates communication, analyzes requirements, and supports decisions.

Business analysis is not limited to software development. It can support operational improvement, organizational change, strategic planning, compliance, and technology initiatives.

### 2.2 Essential Terms

**Business need:** A problem or opportunity that motivates change.

**Stakeholder:** An individual, group, or organization with a relationship to the change, its needs, or its outcomes.

**Requirement:** A usable representation of a need.

**Design:** A representation of a solution.

**Solution:** A specific way of satisfying one or more needs within a context.

**Value:** The worth, importance, or usefulness of something to a stakeholder in a context.

**Context:** The circumstances influencing a change, including organizational, technological, regulatory, and environmental factors.

**Change:** The transformation of an enterprise in response to a need.

### 2.3 Example

A delivery company experiences frequent shipment delays.

* Business need: Improve delivery reliability.
* Stakeholders: Customers, dispatchers, drivers, managers, and suppliers.
* Requirement: Customers need access to current shipment status.
* Design: A tracking interface integrated with shipment data.
* Solution: The implemented tracking capability and associated processes.
* Value: Better customer visibility and potentially fewer status inquiries.

The terms are related but are not interchangeable.

## 3. Core Concepts Review

### 3.1 Need, Requirement, and Design

A need explains why change may be necessary.

A requirement describes something needed to address the need.

A design represents a possible way to satisfy requirements.

Example:

* Need: Reduce fraudulent transactions.
* Requirement: Suspicious transactions must be flagged for review.
* Design: A transaction-monitoring component evaluates defined risk indicators.

### 3.2 Stakeholders

Stakeholders may have different goals, authority, expectations, and concerns.

The analyst identifies relevant stakeholders and supports communication and decision-making.

Stakeholder disagreement should be investigated rather than automatically resolved in favor of the most vocal participant.

### 3.3 Value

Value depends on stakeholder priorities and context.

It may include:

* Revenue growth.
* Cost reduction.
* Better customer experience.
* Improved safety.
* Regulatory compliance.
* Reduced risk.
* Better information.
* Operational reliability.

A solution should be evaluated against the outcomes it is intended to achieve.

## 4. Planning and Monitoring Review

Business analysis planning establishes how the analysis work will be performed.

Monitoring examines progress and whether the approach remains appropriate.

Governance establishes decision-making responsibilities, authority, and controls.

Information management addresses how business analysis information is organized, stored, maintained, accessed, and communicated.

Stakeholder engagement planning determines how stakeholders will participate and receive information.

### Key Distinction

A project plan and a business analysis approach are related but different.

A project plan typically coordinates the overall initiative, including schedule, resources, and delivery activities.

A business analysis approach explains how the analysis work will be performed in its context.

## 5. Elicitation and Collaboration Review

Elicitation is the work of drawing out, discovering, and confirming information relevant to business analysis.

Collaboration involves working with stakeholders and other participants to develop shared understanding and achieve outcomes.

Common elicitation techniques include:

* Interviews.
* Workshops.
* Observation.
* Document analysis.
* Surveys.
* Brainstorming.
* Prototyping.

### Confirming Results

Elicitation does not end when a meeting finishes.

The analyst may need to document findings, confirm understanding, resolve inconsistencies, and follow up on unanswered questions.

### Example

An interview reveals that employees regularly correct customer addresses manually.

The analyst should investigate the cause, determine the business impact, and confirm whether the issue is related to data quality, process design, or system functionality.

The analyst should not immediately assume that a new application is required.

## 6. Requirements Life Cycle Management Review

The five tasks are:

1. Trace Requirements.
2. Maintain Requirements.
3. Prioritize Requirements.
4. Assess Requirements Changes.
5. Approve Requirements.

### Common Exam Distinctions

**Traceability:** Establishes relationships between requirements and related information.

**Maintenance:** Keeps requirements and designs accurate, usable, and relevant.

**Prioritization:** Determines relative importance.

**Change assessment:** Evaluates the implications of a proposed change.

**Approval:** Establishes acceptance by the appropriate authority.

### Example

A stakeholder requests a new reporting feature shortly before a release.

The analyst investigates the reason for the request, identifies affected requirements, assesses cost and schedule impacts, and presents the findings to the appropriate decision-makers.

The analyst should not confuse assessing a change with approving it.

## 7. Strategy Analysis Review

Strategy analysis connects the business need with the current state, desired future state, risks, and change strategy.

### Essential Questions

* What problem or opportunity exists?
* What is happening today?
* What outcome is desired?
* What gaps prevent that outcome?
* What risks could affect the change?
* What approach is most suitable?

### Example

A warehouse experiences a high stockout rate.

The current-state analysis identifies inaccurate inventory records.

The future state defines an acceptable inventory accuracy target.

Gap analysis identifies missing data controls and process weaknesses.

Risk assessment examines migration, operational disruption, and adoption risks.

The change strategy compares process improvements, system integration, training, and alternative solutions.

## 8. Requirements Analysis and Design Definition Review

The six tasks are:

1. Specify and Model Requirements.
2. Verify Requirements.
3. Validate Requirements.
4. Define Requirements Architecture.
5. Define Design Options.
6. Analyze Potential Value and Recommend Solution.

### Verification vs. Validation

Verification asks whether requirements and designs are appropriately expressed.

Validation asks whether they address the underlying business need.

### Example

Requirement: "The system should respond quickly."

Verification identifies ambiguity.

After clarification, the requirement specifies an agreed response-time threshold.

Validation then examines whether that threshold is appropriate for the intended users and business objective.

Both activities contribute to requirement quality and relevance.

## 9. Solution Evaluation Review

Solution evaluation assesses actual performance, limitations, and opportunities to improve value.

Important concepts include:

* Performance measures.
* Baselines.
* Targets.
* Actual results.
* Solution limitations.
* Enterprise limitations.
* Improvement recommendations.
* Financial and non-financial value.

### Example

A company introduces automated invoice processing.

The system functions correctly, but invoices still experience delays because managers approve them infrequently.

The analyst should investigate organizational workflow and approval constraints rather than automatically assuming that the software is defective.

## 10. Techniques Review

| Technique         | Main Purpose                                              |
| ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------- |
| Interview         | Explore stakeholder information and experiences           |
| Workshop          | Collaborate toward a shared outcome                       |
| Observation       | Understand actual behavior and work                       |
| Document analysis | Extract information from existing materials               |
| Brainstorming     | Generate ideas                                            |
| SWOT              | Examine strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats |
| PESTLE            | Analyze external environmental factors                    |
| Five Whys         | Investigate possible root causes                          |
| Process modeling  | Represent activities, decisions, and flows                |
| Data analysis     | Identify patterns and performance issues                  |
| Decision matrix   | Compare alternatives using criteria                       |
| Benchmarking      | Compare performance with a reference point                |

Technique selection should depend on the question being investigated.

## 11. Practice Examination â€” Set A

Select the single best answer for each question.

### Question 1

A company wants to reduce customer complaints about delayed deliveries. What should the Business Analyst establish first?

A. The programming language for a new application.

B. The business need, current situation, and relevant stakeholder concerns.

C. The final user interface design.

D. The vendor contract.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: The analyst should understand the problem and context before committing to a solution.

### Question 2

Which statement best describes a stakeholder?

A. Only a person who pays for a project.

B. Only a software developer.

C. An individual, group, or organization relevant to a change or its outcomes.

D. Only the project manager.

**Correct answer: C**

Explanation: Stakeholders include anyone relevant to the change, its needs, or its outcomes.

### Question 3

A manager requests a new feature, but its business benefit is unclear. What should the analyst do?

A. Accept the request immediately.

B. Investigate the underlying need and expected value.

C. Reject the request because managers cannot propose requirements.

D. Implement the feature without documenting it.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Understanding the need prevents unnecessary or misaligned solution work.

### Question 4

Which activity most directly establishes how business analysis work will be performed?

A. Planning the business analysis approach.

B. Deploying the final solution.

C. Closing every stakeholder account.

D. Retiring all requirements.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: The business analysis approach defines how analysis work will be conducted in its context.

### Question 5

A stakeholder repeatedly provides contradictory information. What is the most appropriate response?

A. Select the most recent statement without discussion.

B. Investigate the inconsistency and confirm the stakeholder's intended meaning.

C. Ignore the stakeholder.

D. Immediately replace the solution.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Conflicting information should be clarified before it is treated as an established requirement.

### Question 6

Which task concerns the relative importance of requirements?

A. Requirements traceability.

B. Requirements prioritization.

C. Requirements maintenance.

D. Requirements approval.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Prioritization helps allocate limited resources according to relative importance.

### Question 7

A new regulation may affect several approved requirements. Which activity should help determine the implications?

A. Requirements change assessment.

B. Stakeholder name collection only.

C. Final report formatting.

D. Brainstorming without evaluation.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Change assessment identifies impacts and supports an informed decision.

### Question 8

A requirement is clearly written but does not address the actual business problem. Which activity is most relevant?

A. Validation.

B. Version control only.

C. Meeting scheduling.

D. Status reporting only.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Validation checks alignment with the underlying business need.

### Question 9

Which model is particularly suitable for representing business activities and decision flows?

A. Process model.

B. Contact list.

C. Salary table only.

D. Project invoice.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Process models describe activities, decisions, and flows of work.

### Question 10

An organization wants to compare building a system with purchasing an existing product. What should it do?

A. Select the option with the newest technology.

B. Evaluate alternatives against relevant value, cost, risk, and feasibility criteria.

C. Ignore stakeholder requirements.

D. Avoid examining the current state.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Design alternatives should be compared using criteria relevant to the business need.

## 12. Practice Examination â€” Set B

### Question 11

A dashboard reports sales accurately, but managers cannot identify why customer retention is falling. What should the analyst consider?

A. Whether the information and measures support the business question.

B. Whether every dashboard should be deleted.

C. Whether reporting frequency is always irrelevant.

D. Whether data quality never matters.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Accurate reporting is not sufficient if the information does not help answer the relevant business question.

### Question 12

Which technique is most appropriate when an analyst wants to understand informal workarounds used by employees?

A. Observation.

B. Market forecasting alone.

C. Budget approval.

D. Organizational branding.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Observation can reveal actual work practices that formal documentation may omit.

### Question 13

A company wants to understand how inflation, legislation, technology, and social trends may affect a strategy. Which technique is appropriate?

A. PESTLE.

B. MoSCoW.

C. Traceability matrix.

D. User story mapping only.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: PESTLE organizes external environmental factors.

### Question 14

A system meets its functional requirements but cannot support expected transaction volumes. What is the likely issue?

A. A performance or scalability limitation.

B. A stakeholder attendance issue only.

C. A business objective automatically achieved.

D. A requirements approval success that eliminates all concerns.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: A solution can provide the required functions while failing non-functional performance expectations.

### Question 15

Which is an example of a business capability?

A. The ability to manage customer orders.

B. A single meeting invitation.

C. A temporary email message.

D. A file's creation timestamp.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: A capability describes what an organization must be able to do.

### Question 16

A project team discovers that users interpret a requirement differently. What should happen next?

A. Clarify the requirement and confirm shared understanding.

B. Allow each user to implement a different interpretation.

C. Ignore the ambiguity until launch.

D. Delete the business objective.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Ambiguity creates inconsistency and should be resolved at the appropriate stage.

### Question 17

Actual performance improves but remains below the target. What should the analyst conclude?

A. No improvement occurred.

B. The solution has improved relative to the baseline, but the target remains unmet.

C. The target has automatically been achieved.

D. All future measurement is unnecessary.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Baselines and targets provide different reference points for evaluation.

### Question 18

Which activity primarily focuses on establishing relationships between requirements and their sources or related artifacts?

A. Traceability.

B. Brainstorming.

C. PESTLE.

D. Estimation.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Traceability links requirements to objectives, sources, designs, tests, and other relevant information.

### Question 19

An organization has limited resources and many competing requirements. Which technique can help categorize requirements into Must, Should, Could, and Won't have this time?

A. MoSCoW.

B. SWOT.

C. Five Whys.

D. PESTLE.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: MoSCoW supports prioritization through four commonly used categories.

### Question 20

What is the most appropriate response when a solution's value is lower than expected?

A. Investigate the causes, evaluate limitations, and recommend evidence-based actions.

B. Assume every stakeholder was wrong.

C. Stop collecting performance data.

D. Automatically replace the entire solution.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Solution evaluation should identify the reasons for the gap and support appropriate improvement decisions.

## 13. Answer Sheet

| Question | Answer | Question | Answer |
| -------- | ------ | -------- | ------ |
| 1        | B      | 11       | A      |
| 2        | C      | 12       | A      |
| 3        | B      | 13       | A      |
| 4        | A      | 14       | A      |
| 5        | B      | 15       | A      |
| 6        | B      | 16       | A      |
| 7        | A      | 17       | B      |
| 8        | A      | 18       | A      |
| 9        | A      | 19       | A      |
| 10       | B      | 20       | A      |

## 14. Self-Assessment

This is a study aid, not an official IIBA passing standard.

* **18â€“20 correct:** Strong performance on this practice set. Review the explanations and test yourself with unfamiliar scenarios.
* **15â€“17 correct:** Good foundation. Revisit concepts that caused confusion.
* **11â€“14 correct:** Review the relevant modules before attempting another practice set.
* **0â€“10 correct:** Rebuild the foundational concepts and practice distinguishing similar terms.

A high score on this set does not guarantee success on the official examination.

## 15. Common Exam Mistakes

### Mistake 1: Choosing a solution too early

Understand the business need and current situation before selecting a solution.

### Mistake 2: Confusing verification with validation

Verification examines the quality of requirements and designs. Validation examines alignment with the business need.

### Mistake 3: Confusing priority with status

A high-priority requirement is not necessarily approved or implemented.

### Mistake 4: Treating stakeholder requests as automatically valid

Investigate the underlying need and expected value.

### Mistake 5: Assuming all problems require technology

Problems may originate from processes, training, governance, data quality, or organizational constraints.

### Mistake 6: Ignoring context

The most suitable technique depends on the problem, stakeholders, available information, and delivery environment.

### Mistake 7: Selecting an answer that sounds sophisticated rather than relevant

Prefer the option that directly addresses the problem described in the scenario.

## 16. Final Revision Strategy

1. Review the definitions in Module 02.
2. Review planning and monitoring concepts in Module 03.
3. Revisit elicitation and collaboration in Module 04.
4. Review requirements life cycle management in Module 05.
5. Revisit current state, future state, risks, and change strategy in Module 06.
6. Review requirements analysis and design definition in Module 07.
7. Revisit solution performance and value in Module 08.
8. Practice selecting techniques in Module 09.
9. Review the perspectives in Module 10.
10. Complete this practice set again without consulting the answers.

## 17. Official Resources

* IIBA main website: https://www.iiba.org/
* ECBA certification: https://www.iiba.org/business-analysis-certifications/ecba/
* BABOK Guide: https://www.iiba.org/standards-and-resources/babok/

Consult these resources for current certification requirements and official exam guidance.

## 18. Final Reminder

Business analysis is about understanding needs, facilitating change, and supporting value creation.

When faced with an unfamiliar scenario, identify the underlying problem, relevant stakeholders, required information, decision to be made, and expected outcome. Then choose the business analysis activity that best addresses the situation.

# 12 â€” ECBA Final Revision Checklist

## 1. Purpose

This document provides a structured final-review checklist for the Entry Certificate in Business Analysis (ECBA).

Use it to:

* Review foundational business analysis concepts.
* Identify knowledge gaps.
* Track revision progress.
* Practice explaining important distinctions.
* Prepare for scenario-based questions.
* Organize your final study sessions.

This checklist is a supplementary study resource, not an official IIBA exam blueprint or substitute for the current ECBA exam syllabus.

## 2. Business Analysis Fundamentals

### Core Definitions

* [ ] Define business analysis.
* [ ] Explain the role of a Business Analyst.
* [ ] Define business need.
* [ ] Define stakeholder.
* [ ] Define requirement.
* [ ] Define design.
* [ ] Define solution.
* [ ] Define value.
* [ ] Define context.
* [ ] Explain the relationship between needs, requirements, designs, and solutions.

### Business Analysis Principles

* [ ] Explain why business analysis focuses on needs rather than immediately selecting solutions.
* [ ] Understand how stakeholders influence business outcomes.
* [ ] Explain why context affects the choice of analysis techniques.
* [ ] Understand why business value should guide decisions.
* [ ] Recognize that business analysis is relevant beyond software development.
* [ ] Explain why assumptions should be identified and investigated.

### Self-Test

Can you explain the difference between a business need, a requirement, and a design using one practical example?

If not, revisit Module 02.

## 3. Business Analysis Planning and Monitoring

### Planning

* [ ] Explain the purpose of business analysis planning.
* [ ] Understand how an analysis approach is selected.
* [ ] Identify the activities included in a business analysis plan.
* [ ] Explain how stakeholder engagement is planned.
* [ ] Understand business analysis governance.
* [ ] Understand information management.
* [ ] Explain why planning should reflect the initiative's context.

### Monitoring

* [ ] Explain the purpose of monitoring business analysis performance.
* [ ] Understand how planned and actual activities can be compared.
* [ ] Identify circumstances that may require an adjustment to the analysis approach.
* [ ] Understand how performance information supports improvement.

### Self-Test

Can you distinguish a business analysis approach from an overall project plan?

If not, revisit Module 03.

## 4. Elicitation and Collaboration

### Elicitation

* [ ] Define elicitation.
* [ ] Explain how preparation supports elicitation.
* [ ] Identify appropriate elicitation techniques.
* [ ] Understand how information is elicited from stakeholders.
* [ ] Explain why elicitation results should be confirmed.
* [ ] Recognize the difference between stated requests and underlying needs.

### Collaboration

* [ ] Explain the purpose of stakeholder collaboration.
* [ ] Understand how workshops support shared understanding.
* [ ] Identify ways to handle conflicting stakeholder perspectives.
* [ ] Understand the importance of clear communication.
* [ ] Explain why elicitation findings should be documented and communicated.

### Technique Selection

* [ ] Interviews.
* [ ] Workshops.
* [ ] Observation.
* [ ] Document analysis.
* [ ] Surveys.
* [ ] Brainstorming.
* [ ] Prototyping.

### Self-Test

Can you select an appropriate elicitation technique for a stakeholder who cannot clearly describe a complicated work process?

If not, revisit Module 04 and Module 09.

## 5. Requirements Life Cycle Management

### Trace Requirements

* [ ] Define traceability.
* [ ] Distinguish backward, forward, and bidirectional traceability.
* [ ] Explain how traceability supports impact analysis.
* [ ] Understand links between requirements, objectives, designs, and tests.

### Maintain Requirements

* [ ] Explain requirements maintenance.
* [ ] Understand version management.
* [ ] Recognize the importance of accurate requirement status.
* [ ] Explain how outdated or inconsistent requirements should be managed.

### Prioritize Requirements

* [ ] Explain why prioritization is necessary.
* [ ] Understand business value, cost, risk, urgency, and dependencies.
* [ ] Apply MoSCoW.
* [ ] Distinguish priority from approval status.

### Assess Requirements Changes

* [ ] Explain change assessment.
* [ ] Identify the potential impacts of a proposed change.
* [ ] Understand how dependencies affect change decisions.
* [ ] Explain why a requested change should not automatically be accepted or rejected.

### Approve Requirements

* [ ] Explain requirements approval.
* [ ] Identify the importance of designated approval authority.
* [ ] Distinguish approval from validation.
* [ ] Understand that approval does not eliminate the need for further analysis.

### Self-Test

Can you explain the difference between tracing a requirement, maintaining it, prioritizing it, assessing a proposed change, and approving it?

If not, revisit Module 05.

## 6. Strategy Analysis

### Current State

* [ ] Explain current-state analysis.

* [ ] Understand existing processes and capabilities.
* [ ] Identify relevant performance baselines.
* [ ] Recognize internal and external drivers of change.

### Future State

* [ ] Identify constraints that influence the desired outcome.

### Gap Analysis

* [ ] Distinguish a gap from a proposed solution.

### Risk Assessment

* [ ] Recognize possible risk responses.

### Change Strategy

* [ ] Understand costs, benefits, risks, and dependencies.
* [ ] Explain why organizational readiness matters.

## 7. Requirements Analysis and Design Definition

## 11. High-Priority Concept Comparisons

Review the following distinctions until you can explain each without referring to your notes.

| Concept A           | Concept B             | Key Distinction                                               |
| ------------------- | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Business need       | Requirement           | Reason for change vs. representation of what is needed        |
| Requirement         | Design                | What is needed vs. a representation of a solution             |
| Stakeholder         | Business Analyst      | Participant or interested party vs. a business analysis role  |
| Elicitation         | Collaboration         | Discovering information vs. working together toward outcomes  |
| Priority            | Status                | Relative importance vs. current management state              |
| Traceability        | Maintenance           | Relationships vs. keeping information accurate and relevant   |
| Verification        | Validation            | Requirement quality vs. alignment with the business need      |
| Current state       | Future state          | Existing conditions vs. desired conditions                    |
| Gap                 | Solution              | Difference to address vs. an approach for addressing it       |
| Solution limitation | Enterprise limitation | Constraint of the solution vs. constraint of the organization |
| Baseline            | Target                | Reference performance vs. desired performance                 |
| KPI                 | Business objective    | Performance measure vs. intended business outcome             |
| Business capability | Business process      | Ability to achieve an outcome vs. activities performed        |
| Risk                | Issue                 | Uncertain event or condition vs. an existing problem or event |

## 12. Scenario-Based Reasoning Checklist

Before selecting an answer to a scenario-based question, ask:

1. **What is the actual problem?** Identify the underlying need rather than accepting the first proposed solution.
2. **Who is affected?** Identify stakeholders and their concerns.
3. **What information is missing?** Determine whether clarification, elicitation, or analysis is needed.
4. **What is the immediate task?** Distinguish planning, elicitation, analysis, change management, approval, and evaluation.
5. **What constraints apply?** Consider time, cost, risk, regulation, and organizational capabilities.
6. **What outcome is desired?** Connect the decision to business value.
7. **What evidence supports the answer?** Prefer evidence-based decisions over unsupported assumptions.
8. **What is the most appropriate next step?** Select the action that addresses the scenario's immediate need.

## 14. Final Readiness Checklist

Before booking or taking the examination, confirm the following.

### Knowledge

* [ ] I can explain the core business analysis terminology.
* [ ] I understand the purpose of the main business analysis activities.
* [ ] I can distinguish commonly confused concepts.

* [ ] I can apply the concepts to unfamiliar scenarios.

### Practice

### Administrative Preparation

* [ ] I have scheduled the examination only when adequately prepared.

Official ECBA information: https://www.iiba.org/business-analysis-certifications/ecba/

## 15. Final Knowledge Test

Try answering these without looking at earlier modules.

1. Why should a Business Analyst investigate the underlying need before recommending a solution?
2. How does traceability support impact analysis?
3. What is the difference between prioritizing a requirement and approving it?
4. Why can a requirement be verified but still fail validation?
5. How does gap analysis support strategy analysis?
6. What is the difference between a solution limitation and an enterprise limitation?
7. When would an interview be preferable to observation?
8. Why should performance targets be compared with both baselines and actual results?
9. How can a weighted decision matrix help compare alternatives, and what are its limitations?
10. Why may an initiative need several business analysis perspectives?

### Suggested Answers

**1.** To ensure that the proposed change addresses the actual business problem and delivers relevant value.

**2.** It establishes relationships between requirements and related objectives, designs, tests, and other artifacts, helping identify affected elements.

**3.** Prioritization establishes relative importance; approval records acceptance by the appropriate authority.

**4.** A requirement can be clear and consistent but still fail to address the real business need.

**5.** It identifies the differences between the existing and desired states, helping determine what change is needed.

**6.** A solution limitation concerns the solution's capabilities; an enterprise limitation concerns organizational conditions such as skills, resources, or policies.

**7.** Interviews are useful for exploring individual experiences and perspectives; observation is useful when actual behavior or informal practices must be understood.

**8.** The baseline establishes the starting point, the target defines desired performance, and actual results show what has been achieved.

**9.** A weighted matrix structures comparisons using criteria and weights, but the results depend on the selected criteria, assumptions, and judgments.

**10.** Complex initiatives may involve strategic capabilities, business processes, data analysis, technology implementation, and iterative delivery simultaneously.

## 16. Final Revision Principles

Remember these foundational principles:

* Understand the need before selecting a solution.
* Identify the stakeholders relevant to the change.
* Select techniques according to the context.
* Keep requirements clear, relevant, and traceable.
* Assess changes before making decisions.
* Verify requirement quality and validate business relevance.
* Compare the current state with the desired future state.
* Evaluate solutions using evidence and appropriate measures.
* Consider organizational limitations as well as technical limitations.
* Connect business analysis decisions to stakeholder needs and value.

## 17. Official Resources

* IIBA: https://www.iiba.org/
* ECBA certification: https://www.iiba.org/business-analysis-certifications/ecba/
* BABOK Guide: https://www.iiba.org/standards-and-resources/babok/

Use official IIBA materials to confirm current exam coverage and certification requirements before your examination.

# FILE: 13_Agile_Business_Analysis.md

# 13 â€” ECBA Requirements Quality and Traceability

## 1. Module Objectives

By the end of this module, you should be able to:

* Explain the characteristics of quality requirements.
* Identify ambiguity, inconsistency, and incompleteness.
* Understand requirements verification and validation.
* Explain requirements traceability and relationships.
* Understand requirement versioning and status management.
* Apply quality and traceability concepts to practical scenarios.

## 2. Introduction

Requirements provide a shared understanding of what stakeholders need from a change. Poor requirements can cause rework, delays, incorrect implementations, and failure to deliver expected business value.

Requirements quality concerns whether requirements are expressed in a way that supports understanding, analysis, implementation, and evaluation.

Traceability concerns the relationships between requirements and other relevant information.

Together, quality management and traceability help teams understand what must be delivered, why it is needed, how it relates to other elements, and whether the intended outcomes have been achieved.

## 3. Characteristics of Quality Requirements

Requirements should be suitable for their intended purpose and audience.

Important characteristics include:

### 3.1 Clear

A requirement should have an understandable meaning.

Poor: "The system should process requests quickly."

Improved: "The system shall display the request confirmation within two seconds for at least 95% of eligible requests under the agreed normal operating load."

The improved requirement provides a measurable expectation. The actual threshold should be agreed with stakeholders.

### 3.2 Complete

A requirement should contain the information necessary for its intended use.

Poor: "The system shall notify customers."

Questions remain: Which customers? Under what conditions? Through which channel? What information must be included?

Completeness is assessed in context. A requirement need not contain every implementation detail, but missing information must not prevent correct interpretation or use.

### 3.3 Consistent

Requirements should not contradict one another.

Example:

* Requirement A: Customers can cancel orders until shipment dispatch.
* Requirement B: Customers cannot cancel orders after payment.

If payment normally occurs before dispatch, these statements may conflict. The analyst must clarify the intended business rules.

### 3.4 Testable

A requirement should support a method for determining whether it has been satisfied.

Poor: "The application shall be user-friendly."

Improved: "At least 90% of representative first-time users shall complete the defined checkout task without assistance during the agreed usability test."

Testability depends on an agreed measurement method and acceptance criteria.

### 3.5 Feasible

A requirement should be achievable within applicable technical, financial, legal, operational, and time constraints.

A requirement that exceeds available infrastructure or budget may require negotiation, alternative designs, or a change in scope.

### 3.6 Necessary

A requirement should contribute to a genuine need, objective, obligation, or justified constraint.

Unnecessary requirements can increase costs and complexity without delivering meaningful value.

### 3.7 Traceable

A requirement should be capable of being linked to relevant sources, objectives, designs, tests, and other artifacts when appropriate.

Traceability supports change assessment and demonstrates why a requirement exists.

### 3.8 Unambiguous

A requirement should not reasonably support multiple conflicting interpretations.

Words such as "fast," "simple," "appropriate," and "efficient" may need clarification.

## 4. Requirements Verification

Verification examines whether requirements and designs are expressed appropriately and meet relevant quality standards.

Verification questions include:

* Is the requirement clear?
* Is it consistent with related requirements?
* Is it sufficiently complete?
* Can it be tested or otherwise assessed?
* Is the terminology used consistently?
* Does it follow applicable documentation standards?

### Example

Requirement: "The system must respond quickly."

A reviewer identifies that "quickly" is subjective.

The requirement is revised to specify a response-time threshold, workload conditions, and measurement method.

This improves its quality and testability.

## 5. Requirements Validation

Validation examines whether requirements and designs address the actual business need and contribute to desired outcomes.

Validation questions include:

* Does this requirement support a business objective?
* Does it address the stakeholder's actual need?
* Is the proposed capability appropriate?
* Does it create unnecessary complexity?
* Will satisfying it contribute to the desired future state?

### Example

A company asks for a sophisticated dashboard.

Investigation reveals that managers primarily need a daily exception report to identify delayed orders.

A simpler reporting capability may satisfy the business need more effectively.

The distinction is important: a requirement can be written perfectly yet still be the wrong requirement.

## 6. Verification Versus Validation

| Aspect           | Verification                        | Validation                                               |
| ---------------- | ----------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| Main concern     | Quality of requirements and designs | Relevance to business needs                              |
| Typical question | Is it expressed correctly?          | Is it the right thing to address the need?               |
| Example          | Clarifying an ambiguous threshold   | Confirming the threshold supports the business objective |
| Potential result | Improved requirement quality        | Revised scope or requirements                            |

Both activities are important and may occur iteratively throughout an initiative.

## 7. Requirements Traceability

### 7.1 Definition

Traceability establishes and maintains relationships between requirements and relevant elements.

Depending on the initiative, these relationships may connect requirements to:

* Business objectives.
* Stakeholder needs.
* Sources.
* Other requirements.
* Designs.
* Development work items.
* Test cases.
* Solution components.
* Business rules.
* Delivered capabilities.

### 7.2 Forward Traceability

Forward traceability follows a requirement toward related downstream elements.

Example:

Business requirement â†’ Detailed requirement â†’ Design element â†’ Test case.

It can help determine whether requirements have been addressed during delivery.

### 7.3 Backward Traceability

Backward traceability follows an element back to its source or justification.

Example:

Test case â†’ Requirement â†’ Stakeholder need â†’ Business objective.

It can help determine why a particular feature or test exists.

### 7.4 Bidirectional Traceability

Bidirectional traceability supports tracing in both directions.

It helps analysts understand both the origins and downstream implications of requirements.

The precise terminology and implementation depend on the organization's traceability approach.

## 8. Traceability Matrix

A traceability matrix records relationships between relevant elements.

Example:

| Requirement ID | Business Objective | Design  | Test Case | Status       |
| -------------- | ------------------ | ------- | --------- | ------------ |
| REQ-001        | OBJ-01             | DES-001 | TC-001    | Approved     |
| REQ-002        | OBJ-01             | DES-002 | TC-002    | In progress  |
| REQ-003        | OBJ-02             | DES-003 | TC-003    | Under review |

The matrix can reveal missing relationships.

For example, an approved requirement without an associated test or evaluation method may require investigation, depending on the requirement type.

A matrix is only useful when its information is accurate and maintained.

## 9. Requirements Versioning

Versioning records changes to requirements over time.

It helps teams understand:

* What changed.
* When it changed.
* Why it changed.
* Who authorized or made the change.
* Which version is current.
* Which related elements may be affected.

Example:

* Version 1.0: Password must contain at least eight characters.
* Version 1.1: Password must contain at least twelve characters.
* Change reason: Updated security policy.

The team should identify affected designs, tests, user guidance, and other dependent elements.

## 10. Requirements Status Management

A requirement may have a status such as:

* Proposed.
* Under review.
* Approved.
* In progress.
* Implemented.
* Verified.
* Rejected.
* Deferred.

These labels are illustrative. Organizations may define different status models.

Status indicates the requirement's current management state; it does not automatically indicate its priority or business value.

A high-priority requirement may still be under review.

## 11. Impact Analysis

Impact analysis examines the consequences of a proposed change.

Traceability helps identify affected requirements, designs, tests, processes, stakeholders, and solution components.

### Example

A company changes the definition of an eligible customer.

The analyst investigates the effects on:

1. Customer eligibility rules.
2. Registration requirements.
3. Existing customer records.
4. Reports.
5. Automated tests.
6. Training materials.
7. Regulatory controls.

This reduces the likelihood of inconsistent changes.

## 12. Practical Case Study

### Scenario: Online Ordering Platform

A retailer wants to introduce order cancellation.

The analyst documents the business need, identifies stakeholder requirements, and creates relationships between requirements and business objectives.

A requirement states:

"Customers can cancel eligible orders before dispatch."

During verification, the team identifies ambiguity around the term "eligible."

During validation, stakeholders confirm that the cancellation capability must reduce customer support requests without creating unnecessary warehouse disruption.

The analyst clarifies the eligibility rules and links them to design elements and test cases.

Later, the business changes its cancellation policy.

Traceability helps identify affected requirements, application behavior, customer communications, and tests.

The team assesses the impact before implementing the change.

## 13. Practice Questions

### Question 1

What is the main purpose of requirements verification?

A. To ensure requirements address the business need regardless of their quality.

B. To assess whether requirements are expressed appropriately and meet quality standards.

C. To approve every requirement automatically.

D. To eliminate stakeholder participation.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Verification examines the quality of requirements and designs.

### Question 2

A requirement is clear and testable but does not address the business problem. Which activity is most relevant?

A. Validation.

B. Version numbering only.

C. Status reporting only.

D. Document formatting.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Validation assesses alignment with the underlying business need.

### Question 3

Which activity helps determine which tests may need modification when a requirement changes?

A. Traceability and impact analysis.

B. Branding.

C. Meeting scheduling.

D. Recruitment.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Traceability helps identify dependent elements affected by a change.

### Question 4

What does a requirement's priority indicate?

A. Its current implementation status.

B. Its relative importance compared with other requirements.

C. Its version number.

D. Its author.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Priority and status represent different dimensions of requirement management.

### Question 5

Which statement best describes backward traceability?

A. Following a requirement to its source or justification.

B. Assigning a developer to every requirement.

C. Automatically implementing a requirement.

D. Removing relationships between requirements.

**Correct answer: A**

Explanation: Backward traceability helps establish the origin and rationale of an element.

### Question 6

Why is version management useful?

A. It prevents all future changes.

B. It records changes and helps identify the current version and affected elements.

C. It removes the need for approval.

D. It guarantees that every requirement is correct.

**Correct answer: B**

Explanation: Version management supports controlled changes and reliable information.

## 14. Revision Checklist

* [ ] Explain quality characteristics of requirements.
* [ ] Identify ambiguous and incomplete requirements.
* [ ] Distinguish verification from validation.
* [ ] Explain forward, backward, and bidirectional traceability.
* [ ] Understand the purpose of a traceability matrix.
* [ ] Explain version and status management.
* [ ] Apply traceability to impact analysis.
* [ ] Identify missing relationships in a requirements scenario.

## 15. Further Study Resources

* IIBA: https://www.iiba.org/
* BABOK Guide: https://www.iiba.org/standards-and-resources/babok/
* ECBA certification: https://www.iiba.org/business-analysis-certifications/ecba/

## 16. Final Exam Reminder

Do not confuse requirement quality with business relevance. Verification examines whether a requirement is appropriately expressed; validation examines whether it addresses the need. Traceability connects requirements with their sources and related artifacts, supporting impact analysis and delivery control.
